Monitoring Blackbox Implementations of Multiparty Session Protocols Thanks: This paper is an extension of [ 16 ] with appendices. This research has been supported by the Dutch Research Council (NWO) under project No. 016.Vidi.189.046 (Unifying Correctness for Communicating Software). Affiliation: University of Groningen, Groningen, The Netherlands
Bas van den Heuvel, Jorge A. Pérez, Rares A. Dobre

Abstract
We present a framework for the distributed monitoring of networks of components that coordinate by message-passing, following multiparty session protocols specified as global types. We improve over prior works by (i) supporting components whose exact specification is unknown (“blackboxes”) and (ii) covering protocols that cannot be analyzed by existing techniques. We first give a procedure for synthesizing monitors for blackboxes from global types, and precisely define when a blackbox correctly satisfies its global type. Then, we prove that monitored blackboxes are sound (they correctly follow the protocol) and transparent (blackboxes with and without monitors are behaviorally equivalent).



1
Introduction

Runtime verification excels at analyzing systems with components that cannot be (statically) checked, such as closed-source and third-party components with unknown/partial specifications
[
2
,
12
]
.
In this spirit, we present a monitoring framework for networks of communicating components.
We adopt
global types
from
multiparty session types

[
17
,
18
]
both to specify protocols and to synthesize monitors.
As we explain next, rather than process implementations, we consider
“blackboxes”
—components whose exact structure is unknown.
Also, aiming at wide applicability, we cover networks of monitored components that implement global types that go beyond the scope of existing techniques.

Session types provide precise specifications of the protocols that components should respect.
It is then natural to use session types as references for distributed monitoring
[
7
,
3
,
21
,
14
,
26
,
20
]
.
In particular, Bocchi
et al.

[
7
,
6
,
9
]
use multiparty session types to monitor networks of
\pi
-calculus processes.
Leveraging notions originally conceived for static verification (such as
global types
and their
projection
onto
local types
), their framework guarantees the correctness of monitored networks with statically and dynamically checked components.

However, existing monitoring techniques based on multiparty session types have two limitations.
One concerns the class of protocols they support; the other is their reliance on fully specified components, usually given as (typed) processes.
That is, definitions of networks assume that a component can be inspected—an overly strong assumption in many cases.
There is then a tension between (i) the assumptions on component structure and (ii) the strength of formal guarantees: the less we know about components, the harder it is to establish such guarantees.

Our approach.

We introduce a new approach to monitoring based on multiparty session types that relies on minimal assumptions on a component’s structure.
As key novelty, we consider
blackboxes
—components with unknown structure but observable behavior—and
networks
of monitored blackboxes that use asynchronous message-passing to implement multiparty protocols specified as global types.

As running example, let us consider the global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(inspired by an example by Scalas and Yoshida
[
25
]
), which expresses an authorization protocol between three participants: server (
s
), client (
c
), and authorization service (
a
).

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}:=\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.s{!}c\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{login}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.c{!}a\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{pwd}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.a{!}s\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{succ}\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0},\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}

(1)

This recursive global type (
\mu X
) specifies that
s
sends to
c
(
s{!}c
) a choice between labels
\mathsf{login}
and
\mathsf{quit}
.
In the
\mathsf{login}
-branch,
c
sends to
a
a password of type
\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle
and
a
notifies
s
whether it was correct, after which the protocol repeats (
X
).
In the
\mathsf{quit}
-branch, the protocol simply ends (
\mathsf{end}
).
As explained in
[
25
]
,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
is not supported by most theories of multiparty sessions, including those in
[
7
,
6
,
9
]
.

P_{c}

\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{c}
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{s}
P_{s}

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{a}
P_{a}

\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,a}
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{s,a}
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}

Figure 1:

Monitoring setup based on the global type (multiparty protocol)
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(
1
).
Each protocol participant has a blackbox (an LTS), attached to a monitor (e.g.
P_{c}
and
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{c}
).
The monitors are synthesized from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(thick arrows).
Relative types (e.g.
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}
) obtained by projection from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(thin gray arrows) are used in this synthesis (dotted arrows).

Figure

1
illustrates our approach to monitoring global types such as
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
.
There is a blackbox per participant, denoted
P_{s}
,
P_{c}
, and
P_{a}
, whose behavior is given by a labeled transition system (LTS).
Each blackbox implements a participant as dictated by
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}

while coupled with a monitor (
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{s}
,
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{c}
, and
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{a}
in
Figure

1
).
Monitors are
synthesized
from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
by relying on
relative types

[
15
]
, which provide local views of the global type: they
specify protocols between
pairs
of participants; hence, in the case of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
, we have three relative types:
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}
,
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,a}
, and
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{s,a}
.

Introduced in
[
15
]
for type-checking communicating components, relative types are instrumental to our approach.
They give a fine-grained view of protocols that is convenient for monitor synthesis.
Relative types explicitly specify
dependencies
between participants, e.g., when the behavior of a participant
p
is the result of a prior choice made by some other participants
q
and
r
.
Treating dependencies as explicit messages is key to ensuring the distributed implementability of protocols that usual multiparty theories cannot support (e.g.,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(
1
)). Our algorithm for monitor synthesis mechanically computes these dependencies from relative types, and exploits them to coordinate monitored blackboxes.

A central ingredient in our technical developments is the notion of
satisfaction
(
definition

8
), which defines when a monitored blackbox conforms to the role of a specific protocol participant.
Building upon satisfaction, we prove
soundness
and
transparency
for networks of monitored blackboxes.
Soundness (
theorem

4.2
) ensures that if each monitored blackbox in a network behaves correctly (according to a global type), then the entire network behaves correctly too.
Transparency (
theorem

4.4
) ensures that monitors do not interfere with the (observable) behavior of their contained blackboxes; it is given in terms of a (weak) behavioral equivalence, which is suitably informed by the actions of a given global type.

Related work.

The literature on distributed runtime verification is vast.
In this setting, the survey by Francalanza
et al.

[
12
]
proposes several classification criteria.
Phrased in terms of their criteria, our work concerns distributed monitoring for asynchronous message-passing.
We work with blackboxes, whose monitors are minimally intrusive: they do not alter behavior, but do contribute to coordination.

The works by Bocchi
et al.

[
7
,
6
,
9
]
and by Scalas and Yoshida
[
25
]
, mentioned above, are a main source of inspiration to us.
The work
[
25
]
highlights the limitations of techniques based on the projection of a global type onto local types: many practical protocols, such as
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
, cannot be analyzed because their projection onto local types is undefined.
With respect to
[
7
,
6
,
9
]
, there are three major differences.
First, Bocchi
et al.
rely on precise specifications of components (
\pi
-calculus processes), whereas we monitor blackboxes (LTSs).
Second, we resort to relative types, whereas they rely on local types; this is a limitation, as just mentioned.
Third, their monitors drop incorrect messages (cf.
[
1
]
) instead of signaling errors, as we do.
Their framework ensures transparency (akin to
theorem

4.4
) and safety, i.e., monitored components do not misbehave.
In contrast, we establish soundness, which is different and more technically involved than safety: our focus is on monitoring blackboxes rather than fully specified components, and soundness concerns correct behavior rather than the absence of misbehavior.

We mention runtime verification techniques based on
binary
session types, a sub-class of multiparty session types.
Bartolo Burlò
et al.

[
3
]
monitor sequential processes that communicate synchronously, prove that ill-typed processes raise errors, and consider also probabilistic session types
[
5
,
4
]
.
Other works couple monitoring session types with
blame assignment
upon protocol violations
[
21
,
14
,
26
,
19
]
.
Jia
et al.

[
21
]
monitor asynchronous session-typed processes.
Gommerstadt
et al.

[
14
,
13
]
extend
[
21
]
with rich refinement-based contracts.
We do not consider blame assignment, but it can conceivably be added by enhancing error signals.

Outline.

Section

2
defines networks of monitored blackboxes and their behavior.

Section

3
defines how to synthesize monitors from global types.

Section

4
defines correct monitored blackboxes, and establishes soundness and transparency.

section

5
concludes the paper. We use colors to improve readability.

The full version of this paper
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
includes an appendix with
additional examples (including the running example from
[
7
]
), a description of a practical toolkit based on this paper, and omitted proofs.

2
Networks of Monitored Blackboxes

We write
P,Q,\ldots
to denote
blackbox processes
(simply
blackboxes
) that implement protocol
participants
(denoted
p,q,\ldots
).
We assume that a blackbox
P
is associated with an LTS that specifies its behavior.
Transitions are denoted
P\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\alpha$}}P^{\prime}
.
Actions
\alpha
, defined in
fig.

2
(top), encompass messages
m
, which can be labeled data but also
dependency messages
(simply
dependencies
).
As we will see, dependencies are useful to ensure the coordinated implementation of choices.
Messages abstract away from values, and include only their type.

Let
\ell
and
T
denote a label and a data type, respectively.

Actions

\displaystyle\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}lr@{}lr@{}}{}::=&\tau&\text{(silent)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&m&\text{(message)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{end}&\text{(end)}\end{array}

Messages

\displaystyle m,n

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}lr@{}}{}::=&p{!}q(\ell\langle T\rangle)&\text{(output)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{!}q(\mskip-4.0mu(\ell)\mskip-4.0mu)&\text{(dep. output, {only for networks})}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{?}q(\ell\langle T\rangle)&\text{(input)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{?}q(\mskip-4.0mu(\ell)\mskip-4.0mu)&\text{(dep. input)}\end{array}

Given
\alpha
, the
recipient in
\alpha
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\recip(p{!}q(\ell\langle T\rangle))

\displaystyle:=\recip(p{!}q(\mskip-4.0mu(\ell)\mskip-4.0mu)):=q

\displaystyle\recip(p{?}q(\ell\langle T\rangle))

\displaystyle:=\recip(p{?}q(\mskip-4.0mu(\ell)\mskip-4.0mu)):=\recip(\tau):=\recip(\mathsf{end}):=\text{undefined}

Let
D,E,\ldots
denote sets of participants;
I,J,\ldots
denote non-empty sets of labels;
X,Y,\ldots
denote recursion variables.

Networks

\displaystyle\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}}{}::=&{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}&\text{(monitored blackbox)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathcal{P}\mathbin{|}\mathcal{Q}&\text{(parallel composition)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{error}_{D}&\text{(error signal)}\end{array}

Monitors

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}lr@{}}{}::=&p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}_{i\in I}&\text{(output)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{!}D(\ell)\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M&\text{(dep.\ output)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{?}q\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}_{i\in I}&\text{(input)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&p{?}q\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{i\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}_{i\in I}&\text{(dep.\ input)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mu X\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}X&\text{(recursion)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{end}&\text{(end)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{error}&\text{(error)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\checkmark&\text{(finished)}\end{array}

The set of
subjects of a network
is as follows:

\displaystyle\subjs({\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]})

\displaystyle:=\{p\}

\displaystyle\subjs(\mathcal{P}{\mathbin{|}}\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle:=\subjs(\mathcal{P})\cup\subjs(\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle\subjs(\mathsf{error}_{D})

\displaystyle:=D

Figure 2:
Actions, messages, networks, and monitors.

A silent transition
\tau
denotes an internal computation.
Transitions
p{!}q(\ell\langle T\rangle)
and
p{?}q(\ell\langle T\rangle)
denote the output and input of a message of type
T
with label
\ell
between
p
and
q
, respectively.
If a message carries no data, we write
\ell\langle\rangle
(i.e., the data type is empty).
Dependency outputs are used for monitors, defined below.

We adopt minimal assumptions about the behavior of blackboxes:

Definition 1
(Assumptions)

We assume the following about LTSs of blackboxes:

•

(Finite
\tau
)

Sequences of
\tau
-transitions are finite.

•

(Input/Output)

There are never input- and output-transitions available at the same time.

•

(End)

There are never transitions after an
\mathsf{end}
-transition.

Example 1

The blackboxes
P_{c}
,
P_{s}
,
P_{a}
implement
c
,
s
,
a
, respectively, in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(
1
) with the following LTSs:
P_{c}
P_{c}^{\mathsf{q}}
P_{c}^{\mathsf{e}}
P_{c}^{\,\mathsf{l}}
c{?}s(\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle)
\mathsf{end}
c{?}s(\mathsf{login}\langle\rangle)
c{!}a(\mathsf{pwd}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle)
P_{s}
P_{s}^{\mathsf{q}}
P_{s}^{\mathsf{e}}
P_{s}^{\,\mathsf{l}}
s{!}c(\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle)
\mathsf{end}
s{!}c(\mathsf{login}\langle\rangle)
s{?}a(\mathsf{succ}\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle)
P_{a}
P_{a}^{\mathsf{q}_{s}}
P_{a}^{\mathsf{q}_{c}}
P_{a}^{\mathsf{e}}
P_{a}^{\,\mathsf{l}_{s}}
P_{a}^{\,\mathsf{l}_{c}}
P_{a}^{\mathsf{p}}
a{?}s(\mskip-4.0mu(\mathsf{quit})\mskip-4.0mu)
a{?}c(\mskip-4.0mu(\mathsf{quit})\mskip-4.0mu)
\mathsf{end}
a{?}s(\mskip-4.0mu(\mathsf{login})\mskip-4.0mu)
a{?}c(\mskip-4.0mu(\mathsf{login})\mskip-4.0mu)
a{?}c(\mathsf{pwd}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle)
a{!}s(\mathsf{succ}\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle)

All three LTSs above respect the assumptions in
Definition

1
.
On the other hand, the following LTS violates all three assumptions; in particular, there are an input- and an output-transition simultaneously enabled at
Q
:
Q
Q^{\mathsf{q}}
Q^{\mathsf{p}}
c{?}s(\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle)
\mathsf{end}
c{!}a(\mathsf{pwd}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle)
\tau

Blackboxes communicate asynchronously, using buffers (denoted
\vec{m}
): ordered sequences of messages, with the most-recently received message on the left.
The empty buffer is denoted
\varepsilon
.
When a blackbox does an input transition, it attempts to read the message from its buffer.
An output transition places the message in the recipient’s buffer; to accommodate this, we mark each blackbox with the participant they implement.
The result is a
buffered blackbox
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}
.

By convention, the buffer of
p
contains output messages with recipient
p
.
We allow the silent reordering of messages with different senders; this way, e.g., given
q\neq r
,
\vec{m},q{!}p(\ell\langle T\rangle),r{!}p(\ell^{\prime}\langle T^{\prime}\rangle),\vec{n}
and
\vec{m},r{!}p(\ell^{\prime}\langle T^{\prime}\rangle),q{!}p(\ell\langle T\rangle),\vec{n}
are the same.

Having defined standalone (buffered) blackboxes, we now define how they interact in
networks
.
We couple each buffered blackbox with a
monitor

\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M
, which has its own buffer
\vec{n}
.
The result is a
monitored blackbox
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}
.

Monitors define finite state machines that accept sequences of incoming and outgoing messages, as stipulated by some protocol.
An
error
occurs when a message exchange does not conform to such protocol.
Additionally, monitors support the dependencies mentioned earlier: when a blackbox sends or receives a message, the monitor broadcasts the message’s label to other monitored blackboxes such that they can receive the chosen label and react accordingly.

Networks, defined in
fig.

2
(bottom), are compositions of monitored blackboxes and
error signals
.
An error signal
\mathsf{error}_{D}
replaces a monitored blackbox when its monitor detects an error involving participants in the set
D
.
Indeed, a participant’s error will propagate to the other monitored blackboxes in a network.

Output and (dependency) input monitors check outgoing and incoming (dependency) messages, respectively.
Output dependency monitors
p{!}D(\ell)\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M
broadcast
\ell
to the participants in
D
.
Recursive monitors are encoded by recursive definitions (
\mu X\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M
) and recursive calls (
X
). The
\mathsf{end}
monitor waits for the buffered blackbox to end the protocol.
The
\mathsf{error}
monitor denotes an inability to process received messages; it will be useful when the sender and recipient of an exchange send different dependency messages.
The finished monitor
\checkmark
is self-explanatory.

We now define the behavior of monitored blackboxes in networks:

Definition 2
(LTS for Networks)

We define an
LTS for networks
, denoted
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\alpha$}}\mathcal{Q}
, by the rules in
fig.

3
(
fig.

3
) with actions
\alpha
as in
fig.

2
(top).

We write
\mathcal{P}\Rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
to denote a sequence of zero or more
\tau
-transitions
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}\mathcal{Q}
, and
we write
\mathcal{P}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.04861pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.84029pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}
to denote that there do not exist
\alpha,\mathcal{Q}
such that
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\alpha$}}\mathcal{Q}
.

Figure 3:
Definition 2
(LTS for Networks)
(
definition

2
).

fig.

3
gives four groups of rules, which we briefly discuss.
The Transition group [
buf
-
\ast
] defines the behavior of a buffered blackbox in terms of the behavior of the blackbox it contains; note that input transitions are hidden as
\tau
-transitions.
The Transition group [
mon
-
\ast
] defines the behavior of a monitored blackbox when the behavior of the enclosed buffered blackbox concurs with the monitor; again, input transitions are hidden as
\tau
-transitions.

When the behavior of the buffered blackbox does not concur with the monitor, the Transition group [
error
-
\ast
] replaces the monitored blackbox with an error signal.
Transition [
par-error
] propagates error signals to parallel monitored blackboxes.
If a network parallel to the monitored blackbox of
p
has an outgoing message with recipient
p
, Transition [
out-mon-buf
] places this message in the buffer of the monitored blackbox as a
\tau
-transition.
Transition [
par
] closes transitions under parallel composition, as long as the recipient in the action of the transition (
\recip(\alpha)
) is not a subject of the composed network (
\subjs(\mathcal{Q})
, the participants for which monitored blackboxes and error signals appear in
\mathcal{Q}
).
Transition [
cong
] closes transitions under
\equiv
, which denotes a congruence that defines parallel composition as commutative and associative.

Figure

4
shows transitions of correct/incorrect communications in networks.

3
Monitors for Blackboxes Synthesized from Global Types

In theories of multiparty session types
[
17
,
18
]
,
global types
conveniently describe message-passing protocols between sets of participants from a vantage point.
Here we use them as specifications for monitors in networks (
algorithm

2
);
for a local view of such global protocols we use
relative types

[
15
]
, which describe the interactions and dependencies between
pairs
of participants.

Definition 3
(Global and Relative Types)

Global types

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}lr@{}}{}::=&p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}_{i\in I}&\text{(exchange)}~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{end}&\text{(end)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}X&\text{(recursion)}\end{array}

Relative types

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}lr@{}lr@{}}{}::=&p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}&\text{(exchange)}\!\!~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mathsf{end}&\text{(end)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&(p{!}r){!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}&\text{(output dep.)}\!\!~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&\mu X\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}X&\text{(recursion)}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}&(p{?}r){!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}&\text{(input dep.)}\end{array}

We write
\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)
to denote the set of participants involved in exchanges in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.

The global type
p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{i}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}_{i\in I}
specifies that
p
sends to
q
some
j\in I
with
T_{j}
, continuing as
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{j}
.
A relative type specifies a protocol between a pair of participants, say
p
and
q
.
The type

For both global and relative types, tail-recursion is defined with recursive definitions
\mu X
and recursive calls

, and
\mathsf{end}
specifies the end of the protocol.

Algorithm 1

Definition 4
(Relative Projection)
of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
onto
p
and
q
(Def.
4
).

The projection of an exchange onto
(p,q)
is an exchange if
p
and
q
are sender and recipient (
algorithms

1
and
1
).
Otherwise, if the protocol between
p
and
q
does not depend on the exchange (the projections of all branches are equal), the projection is the union of the projected branches (
algorithm

1
).
The union of relative types, denoted
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\cup\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}
, is defined only on identical relative types (e.g.,
p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}\cup p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}=p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}
; see
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
for a formal definition).
If there is a dependency and
p
or
q
is sender/recipient, the projection is a dependency (
algorithms

1
and
1
).
Projection is undefined if there is a dependency but
p
nor
q
is involved.

The projection of
\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
is a relative type starting with a recursive definition, provided that the projection of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
onto
(p,q)
contains an exchange or nested recursive call (
algorithm

1
) to avoid recursion with only dependencies;
otherwise, the projection returns
\mathsf{end}
(
algorithm

1
).
The projections of recursive calls and
\mathsf{end}
are homomorphic (
algorithms

1
and
1
).

.

Not all global types are sensible.
A valid global type may, e.g., require a participant
p
to have different behaviors, depending on a choice that
p
is unaware of (see, e.g.,
[
8
]
).
In the following, we work only with
well-formed
global types:

Definition 5
(Well-formedness)

We say a global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
is
well-formed
if and only if,
for all pairs of participants
p\neq q\in\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)
, the projection
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{\rangle}(p,q)
is defined,
and
all recursion in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
is non-contractive (e.g.,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\neq\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.X
) and bound.

Our running example
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
(
1
) is well-formed in the above sense; also, as explained in
[
25
]
,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
is
not
well-formed in most theories of multiparty sessions (based on projection onto local types).
As such,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
goes beyond the scope of such theories.

Synthesizing monitors.

Next, we define a procedure to synthesize monitors for the participants of global types. This procedure detects dependencies as follows:

Definition 6
(Dependence)

Given a global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
, we say
p

depends on

q
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
, denoted
p\mathbin{\@depsOn}q\mathbin{\@in}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
, if and only if

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G=s{!}r\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{i}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}_{i\in I}\wedge p\notin\{s,r\}\wedge q\in\{s,r\}\wedge\exists i,j\in I.~\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{i}\mathbin{\rangle}(p,q)\neq\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{j}\mathbin{\rangle}(p,q)
.

Thus,
p\mathbin{\@depsOn}q\mathbin{\@in}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
holds if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
is an exchange involving
q
but not
p
, and the relative projections of at least two branches of the exchange are different.

Algorithm 2

Definition 7
(Synthesis of Monitors from Global Types)
(
definition

7
).

Initially,
D=\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)\setminus\{p\}
.
The monitor for
p
of an exchange where
p
is sender (resp. recipient) is an output (resp. input) followed in each branch by a dependency output, using
Definition 6
(Dependence)
to compute the participants with dependencies (
algorithms

2
and
2
).
If
p
is not involved, we detect a dependency for
p
with
Definition 6
(Dependence)
.
In case
p
depends on sender/recipient but not both, the monitor is a dependency input (
algorithms

2
and
2
).
If
p
depends on sender
and
recipient, the monitor contains two consecutive dependency inputs (
algorithm

2
); when the two received labels differ, the monitor enters an
\mathsf{error}
-state.
When there is no dependency for
p
, the monitor uses an arbitrary branch (
algorithm

2
).
To synthesize a monitor for
\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
, the algorithm uses projection to compute

with participants having exchanges with
p

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
(cf.
algorithm

1

algorithm

).
If
D^{\prime}
is non-empty, the monitor starts with a recursive definition (
algorithm

2
) and the algorithm continues with
D^{\prime}
; otherwise, the monitor is
\mathsf{end}
(
algorithm

2
).
The monitors of
X
and
\mathsf{end}
are homomorphic (

and

Let us use

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{a}

\displaystyle:=\gtToMon(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}},a,\{c,s\})

\displaystyle=\mu X\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.a{?}s\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\left\{\mskip-5.0mu\middle\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{login}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.a{?}c\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\left\{\mskip-5.0mu\middle\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{login}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}a{?}c\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{\mathsf{pwd}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.a{!}\emptyset(\mathsf{pwd})\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\\
a{!}s\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{\mathsf{succ}\langle\mathsf{bool}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.a{!}\emptyset(\mathsf{succ})\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.X\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42},\end{array}\\
\mathsf{quit}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\mathsf{error}\end{array}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\middle\}\mskip-5.0mu\right\}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42},\\
\mathsf{quit}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.a{?}c\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{\mathsf{quit}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42},\mathsf{login}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\mathsf{error}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}\end{array}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\middle\}\mskip-5.0mu\right\}

4
Properties of Correct Monitored Blackboxes

Given a global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
, we establish the precise conditions under which a network of monitored blackboxes correctly implements
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.
That is, we define how the monitored blackbox
\mathcal{P}
of a participant
p
of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
should behave, i.e., when
\mathcal{P}

satisfies
the role of
p
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
(
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
,
definition

8
).
We then prove two important properties of networks of monitored blackboxes that satisfy a given global type:

Soundness:

The network behaves correctly according to the global type (
theorem

4.2
);

Transparency:

The monitors interfere minimally with buffered blackboxes (
theorem

4.4
).

As we will see in
section

4.2
, satisfaction is exactly the condition under which a network
\mathcal{P}
is sound with respect to a global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.

4.1
Satisfaction

Our aim is to attest that
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the role of
p
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
if it meets certain conditions on the behavior of monitored blackboxes with respect to the protocol.
As we have seen, the role of
p
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
is determined by projection.
Satisfaction is then a relation
\mathcal{R}
between (i) monitored blackboxes and (ii) maps from participants
q\in\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)\setminus\{p\}
to relative types between
p
and
q
, denoted
\mathtt{RTs}
;
\mathcal{R}
must contain
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs})
with relative projections of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.
Given any
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathtt{RTs}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{R}
, the general idea of satisfaction is (i) that an output to
q
by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
means that
\mathtt{RTs}^{\prime}(q)
is a corresponding exchange from
p
to
q
, and (ii) that if there is a
q
such that
\mathtt{RTs}^{\prime}(q)
is an exchange from
q
to
p
then
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
behaves correctly afterwards.

In satisfaction, dependencies in relative types require care.
For example, if
\mathtt{RTs}^{\prime}(q)
is an exchange from
p
to
q
and
\mathtt{RTs}^{\prime}(r)
is a dependency on this exchange, then
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
must first send a label to
q
and then send
the same label
to
r
.
Hence, we need to track the labels chosen by the monitored blackbox for later reference.
To this end, we uniquely identify each exchange in a global type by its
location

\vec{\ell}
: a sequence of labels denoting the choices leading to the exchange.
Projection then uses these locations to annotate each exchange and recursive definition/call in the relative type it produces.
Because projection skips independent exchanges (
algorithm

1
,
algorithm

1
), some exchanges and recursive definitions/calls may be associated with multiple locations; hence, they are annotated with
sets
of locations, denoted
\mathbb{L}
.
Satisfaction then tracks choices using a map from sets of locations to labels, denoted
\mathtt{Lbls}
.
Projection with location annotations is formally defined in
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
, along with a corresponding definition for unfolding recursion.

Before defining satisfaction, we set up some useful notation for type signatures, locations, relative types, and maps.

Notation 4.1

Let
\bm{P}
denote the set of all participants,
\bm{R}
the set of all relative types,
\bm{N}
the set of all networks, and
\bm{L}
the set of all labels.

Notation
\Pow(S)
denotes the powerset of
S
.
Given a set
S
, we write
\vec{S}
to denote the set of all sequences of elements from
S
.
We write
\mathbb{L}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
to stand for
\mathbb{L}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
We write
\mathbb{L}\leq\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
if every
\vec{\ell}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
is prefixed by some
\vec{\ell}\in\mathbb{L}
.

In relative types, we write
\lozenge
to denote either
{!}
or
{?}
.
We write
\unfold(\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)
for the inductive unfolding of
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
if
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
starts with recursive definitions, and for
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
itself otherwise.
We write
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}
whenever
\unfold(\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)=\unfold(\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime})
.

We shall use monospaced fonts to denote maps (such as
\mathtt{RTs}
and
\mathtt{Lbls}
).
We often define maps using the notation of injective relations.
Given a map
\mathtt{M}
, we write
(x,y)\in\mathtt{M}
to denote that
x\in\dom(\mathtt{M})
and
\mathtt{M}(x)=y
.
We write
\mathtt{M}[x\mapsto y^{\prime}]
to denote the map obtained by adding to
\mathtt{M}
an entry for
x
pointing to
y^{\prime}
, or updating an already existing entry for
x
.
Maps are partial unless stated otherwise.

Definition 8
(Satisfaction)

A relation
\mathcal{R}
is
sat-signed
if its signature is
\bm{N}\times(\bm{P}\rightarrow\bm{R})\times(\Pow(\vec{\bm{L}})\rightarrow\bm{L})
.
We define the following properties of relations:

•

A sat-signed relation
\mathcal{R}

holds at
p
if it satisfies the conditions in
fig.

5
.

•

A sat-signed relation
\mathcal{R}

progresses at
p
if for every
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
, we have
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\alpha$}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for some
\alpha
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, given that one of the following holds:

–

\mathtt{RTs}\neq\emptyset
and, for every
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
,
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}\mathsf{end}
;

–

There is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
such that (i)
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}p{!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}
or
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}(p\lozenge r){!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}
, and (ii) for every
(q^{\prime},\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime})\in\mathtt{RTs}\setminus\{(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\}
, either

\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}\mathrel{\circeq}\mathsf{end}

or
\unfold(\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime})
has locations
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
with
\mathbb{L}\leq\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
.

•

A sat-signed relation
\mathcal{R}
is a
satisfaction at
p
if it holds and progresses at
p
.

We write
\mathcal{R}\vDash^{\mathtt{Lbls}}\mathcal{P}\triangleright\mathtt{RTs}\mathbin{@}p
if
\mathcal{R}
is a satisfaction at
p
with
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
, and

\mathcal{R}\vDash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\mathtt{RTs}\mathbin{@}p
when
\mathtt{Lbls}
is empty.
We omit
\mathcal{R}
to indicate such
\mathcal{R}
exists.

Given
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
, all the following conditions hold:

1.

(Tau)

If
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\tau$}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
then
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
.

2.

(End)

If
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\mathsf{end}$}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
then, for every
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
,

\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}\mathsf{end}
,

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.04861pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.84029pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rightarrow}}}
, and
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}
.

3.

(Output)

If
\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$p{!}q(j\langle T_{j}\rangle)$}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
then

\mathtt{RTs}(q)\mathrel{\circeq}p{!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}

with
j\in I
, and
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])\in\mathcal{R}
.

4.

(Input)

If there is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}q{!}p^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}
,
then

\mathcal{P}={\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}
,
and, for every
j\in I
,

({\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}q{!}p(j\langle T_{j}\rangle),\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]},\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])\in\mathcal{R}
.

5.

(Dependency output)

If
\mathcal{P}\mkern-3.0mu\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$p{!}q(\mskip-4.0mu(j)\mskip-4.0mu)$}}\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
then

\mathtt{RTs}(q)\mathrel{\circeq}(p\lozenge r){!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}

with
j\in I
,
there is
(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},j)\in\mathtt{Lbls}
such that
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}
,
and
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
.

6.

(Dependency input)

If there is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
s.t.
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}(q\lozenge r){!}p^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}
,
then
\mathcal{P}={\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}
, and either of the following holds:

•

(Fresh label)
there is no
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in\dom(\mathtt{Lbls})
such that
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}
, and, for every
j\in I
,
({\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}q{!}p(\mskip-4.0mu(j)\mskip-4.0mu),\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]},\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])\in\mathcal{R}
;

•

(Known label)
there is
(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},j)\in\mathtt{Lbls}
such that
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}
and
j\in I
, and

({\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}q{!}p(\mskip-4.0mu(j)\mskip-4.0mu),\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]},\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Figure 5:
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
: conditions under which
\mathcal{R}
holds at
p
(
definition

8
).

Satisfaction requires
\mathcal{R}
to hold at
p
: each
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
enjoys the conditions in
fig.

5
, discussed next, which ensure that
\mathcal{P}
respects the protocols in
\mathtt{RTs}
.

(
Tau
) allows
\tau
-transitions without affecting
\mathtt{RTs}
and
\mathtt{Lbls}
.
(
End
) allows an
\mathsf{end}
-transition, given that all relative types in
\mathtt{RTs}
are
\mathsf{end}
.
The resulting state should not transition, enforced by empty
\mathtt{RTs}
and
\mathtt{Lbls}
.

(
Output
) allows an output-transition with a message to
q
, given that
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
is a corresponding output by
p
.
Then,
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
updates to the continuation of the appropriate branch and
\mathtt{Lbls}
records the choice under the locations of
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
.

(
Input
) triggers when there is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
is a message from
q
to
p
.
Satisfaction targets the behavior of
\mathcal{P}
on its own, so we simulate a message sent by
q
.
The resulting behavior is then analyzed after buffering any such message;
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
is updated to the continuation of the corresponding branch.
As for outputs,
\mathtt{Lbls}
records the choice at the locations of
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
.

(
Dependency Output
) allows an output-transition with a dependency message to
q
, given that
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
is a corresponding dependency output by
p
with locations
\mathbb{L}
.
The message’s label should be recorded in
\mathtt{Lbls}
at some
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
that shares a location with
\mathbb{L}
: here
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
relates to a past exchange between
p
and some
r
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
from which the dependency output in
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
originates.
This ensures that the dependency output is preceded by a corresponding exchange, and that the dependency output carries the same label as originally chosen for the preceding exchange.
Afterwards,
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
is updated to the continuation of the appropriate branch.

(
Dependency Input
) triggers when there is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
is a dependency exchange from
q
to
p
, forwarding a label exchanged between
q
and
r
.
As in the input case, a message from
q
is simulated by buffering it in
\mathcal{P}
.
In this case,
\mathtt{RTs}(r)
could be a dependency exchange from
r
to
p
, originating from the same exchange between
q
and
r
in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.
To ensure that the buffered messages contain the same label, we distinguish “fresh” and “known” cases.
In the fresh case, we consider the first of the possibly two dependency exchanges: there is no
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in\dom(\mathtt{Lbls})
that shares a location with the locations
\mathbb{L}
of
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
.
Hence, we analyze each possible dependency message, updating
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
appropriately and recording the choice in
\mathtt{Lbls}
.
The known case then considers the second dependency exchange: there is a label in
\mathtt{Lbls}
at
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
that shares a location with
\mathbb{L}
.
Hence, we buffer a message with the same label, and update
\mathtt{RTs}(q)
accordingly.

Satisfaction also requires
\mathcal{R}
to progress at
p
, for each
(\mathcal{P},\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})\in\mathcal{R}
making sure that
\mathcal{P}
does not idle whenever we are expecting a transition from
\mathcal{P}
.
There are two cases.
(1) If all relative types in
\mathtt{RTs}
are
\mathsf{end}
, we expect an
\mathsf{end}
-transition.
(2) If there is a relative type in
\mathtt{RTs}
that is a (dependency) output,
we expect an output transition.
However,
\mathcal{P}
may idle if it is waiting for a message: there is
(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R
is a (dependency) input originating from an exchange in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
that precedes the exchange related to the output.

Definition 9
(Satisfaction for Networks)

Let us write
\RTsOf(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p)
to denote the set
\{(q,\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{\rangle}(p,q))\mid q\in\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)\setminus\{p\}\}
.
Moreover, we write

•

\mathcal{R}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{@}p
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M=\gtToMon(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p,\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)\setminus\{p\})
and
\mathcal{R}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\RTsOf(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p)\mathbin{@}p
.
We omit
\mathcal{R}
to say such
\mathcal{R}
exists.

•

\vDash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
if and only if
\mathcal{P}\equiv{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\prod}_{p\in\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{p}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{p}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}
and, for every
p\in\prt(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G)
,
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{p}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{p}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{@}p
.

Example 5

The following satisfaction assertions hold with implementations, relative types, and monitors from
Examples

1
,
2
and
4
, respectively:

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}c\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(s,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}),(a,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,a})\}\mathbin{@}c

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}s\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(c,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}),(a,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{s,a})\}\mathbin{@}s

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}a\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(c,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,a}),(s,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{s,a})\}\mathbin{@}a

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}c\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}s\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}a\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}

We also have:

\nvDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}c\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\mu X\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.c{?}s\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\{\mskip-5.0mu\{\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}\}\mskip-5.0mu\}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}\mathbin{@}c
. This is because

\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{c,s}
specifies that
s
may send
\mathsf{login}
to
c
, which this monitor would not accept.

4.2
Soundness

Our
first result
is that satisfaction is sound with respect to global types: when a network of monitored blackboxes satisfies a global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
(
definition

9
), any path of transitions eventually reaches a state that satisfies another global type reachable from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.
Hence, the satisfaction of the individual components that a network comprises is enough to ensure that the network behaves as specified by the global type.
Reachability between global types is defined as an LTS:

Definition 10
(LTS for Global Types)

We define an LTS for global types, denoted
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\ell$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
, by the following rules:

j\in I

p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{i}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$j$}}$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{j}

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\{\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G/X\}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\ell$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}

\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\ell$}}$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}

Given
\vec{\ell}=\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
, we write
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\vec{\ell}$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
to denote
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\ell_{1}$}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\ell_{n}$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.2
(Soundness)

If
\vDash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
(
Definition

9
) and
\mathcal{P}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{0}
then there exist
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime},\vec{\ell},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.5pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\vec{\ell}$}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, and
\vDash\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
.

We sketch the proof of
theorem

4.2
(see
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
for details).
We prove a stronger statement that starts from a network
\mathcal{P}
that satisfies an intermediate
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{0}
reachable from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G
.
This way, we apply induction on the number of transitions between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, relating the transitions of the network to the transitions of
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{0}
one step at a time by relying on
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
.
Hence, we inductively “consume” the transitions between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}
until we have passed through
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and end up in a network satisfying
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
reachable from
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{0}
.
We use an auxiliary lemma to account for global types with
independent exchanges
, such as
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}=p{!}q\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\ell\langle T\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.r{!}s\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\ell^{\prime}\langle T^{\prime}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}
.
In
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
, the exchange involving
(p,q)
is unrelated to that involving
(r,s)
, so they occur concurrently in a network implementing
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}
.
Hence, the transitions from
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
might not follow the order specified in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{0}
.
The lemma ensures that concurrent (i.e., unrelated) transitions always end up in the same state, no matter the order.
This way we show transitions from
\mathcal{P}
in the order specified in
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{0}
, which we restore to the observed order using the lemma when we are done.

theorem

4.2
implies that any
\mathcal{P}
that satisfies some global type is
error free
, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}
never reduces to a network containing
\mathsf{error}_{D}
(stated and proved formally in the full version of this paper
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
).

4.3
Transparency

The task of monitors is to observe and verify behavior
with minimal interference
: monitors should be
transparent
.
Transparency is usually expressed as a bisimulation between a monitored and unmonitored component
[
24
,
11
,
7
,
1
]
.

Our
second result
is thus a transparency result.
For it to be informative, we assume that we observe the (un)monitored blackbox as if it were running in a network of monitored blackboxes that adhere to a given global protocol.
This way, we can assume that received messages are correct, such that the monitor does not transition to an error signal.
To this end, we enhance the
Definition 2
(LTS for Networks)
:

1.

As in
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
, we consider (un)monitored blackboxes on their own.
Hence, we need a way to simulate messages sent by other participants.
Otherwise, a blackbox would get stuck waiting for a message and the bisimulation would hold trivially.
We thus add a transition that buffers messages.
Similar to
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
(
Input
) and (
Dependency Input
), these messages cannot be arbitrary; we parameterize the enhanced LTS by an
oracle
that determines which messages are allowed as stipulated by a given global type.

2.

Besides observing and verifying transitions, our monitors additionally send dependency messages.
This leads to an asymmetry in the behavior of monitored blackboxes and unmonitered blackboxes, as the latter do not send dependency messages.
Hence, we rename dependency output actions to
\tau
.

We now define the enhanced LTS for networks, after setting up some notation.

Notation 4.3

Let
\bm{A}
denote the set of all actions.
Given
\Omega:\Pow(\vec{\bm{A}})
, we write
\alpha+\Omega
to denote the set containing every sequence in
\Omega
prepended with
\alpha
.
We write
\Omega(\alpha)=\Omega^{\prime}
iff
\alpha+\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
and there is no
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
such that
\alpha+\Omega^{\prime}\subset\alpha+\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Omega
.

Definition 11
(Enhanced LTS for Networks)

We define an
enhanced LTS for Networks
, denoted
\mathcal{P}\mathrel{{}_{\Omega}{\mathrel{\hbox to12.89pt{\vbox to12.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.4438pt\lower-7.75134pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Figure 6:
Definition 11
(Enhanced LTS for Networks)
(

11
).

Thus, Transitions [
buf
-
\ast
] simulate messages from other participants, consulting
\Omega
and transforming it into
\Omega^{\prime}
.
Transition [
dep
] renames dependency outputs to
\tau
.
Transition [
no-dep
] passes any other transitions, updating
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
accordingly.

We now define a weak bisimilarity on networks, governed by oracles.

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}_{\Omega_{2}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
, and
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{2},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}
; and (2) The symmetric analog.

We say
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are
bisimilar
with respect to
\Omega
, denoted
\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\approx_{\Omega}}\mathcal{Q}
, if there exists a bisimulation
\mathcal{B}
such that
(\mathcal{P},\Omega,\mathcal{Q})\in\mathcal{B}
.

Clause 1 says that
\mathcal{Q}
can mimic a transition from
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, possibly after
\tau
- and [
buf
]-transitions.
We then allow
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to “catch up” on those additional transitions, after which the results are bisimilar (under a new oracle); Clause 2 is symmetric.
Additional [
buf
]-transitions are necessary: an unmonitored blackbox can read messages from its buffer directly, whereas a monitor may need to move messages between buffers first.
If the monitor first needs to move messages that are not in its buffer yet, we need to add those messages with [
buf
]-transitions.
The unmonitored blackbox then needs to catch up on those additional messages.

Similar to
Theorem 4.2
(Soundness)
,
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
defines the conditions under which we prove transparency of monitors.
Moreover, we need to define the precise oracle under which bisimilarity holds.
This oracle is defined similarly to
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
: it depends on actions observed, relative types (in
\mathtt{RTs}
), and prior choices (in
\mathtt{Lbls}
).

Definition 13
(Label Oracle)

The
label oracle
of participant
p
under
\mathtt{RTs}:\bm{P}\rightarrow\bm{R}
and
\mathtt{Lbls}:\Pow(\vec{\bm{L}})\rightarrow\bm{L}
, denoted
\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})
is defined in
fig.

7
.

To improve readability, below we write ‘
{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[x\in S\ldots]
’ instead of ‘
{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}_{x\in S\ldots}
’.

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}p{!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}]~{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[j\in I]

\displaystyle\quad p{!}q(j\langle T_{j}\rangle)+\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])

(Output)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}q{!}p^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\langle T_{i}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}]~{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[j\in I]

\displaystyle\quad p{?}q(j\langle T_{j}\rangle)+\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])

(Input)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}(q\lozenge r){!}p^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}\wedge{\not\exists}\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in\dom(\mathtt{Lbls}).~\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}]~{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[j\in I]

\displaystyle\quad p{?}q(j)+\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls}[\mathbb{L}\mapsto j])

(Fresh Dependency Input)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}(q\lozenge r){!}p^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}\wedge\exists(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},j)\in\mathtt{Lbls}.~\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}\wedge j\in I]

\displaystyle\quad p{?}q(j)+\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls})

(Known Dependency Input)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}[(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}(p\lozenge r){!}q^{\mathbb{L}}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace i\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{i}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace_{i\in I}\wedge\exists(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},j)\in\mathtt{Lbls}.~\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\mathbin{\Cap}\mathbb{L}\wedge j\in I]

\displaystyle\quad\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs}[q\mapsto\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R_{j}],\mathtt{Lbls})

(Dependency Output)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle\mathsf{end}+\LO(p,\emptyset,\emptyset)\quad[\text{only if }\forall(q,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R)\in\mathtt{RTs}.~\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R\mathrel{\circeq}\mathsf{end}]

(End)

\displaystyle\cup~

\displaystyle\tau+\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})

(Tau)

Figure 7:
Definition of the
Definition 13
(Label Oracle)
(
definition

13
),
\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})
.

The label oracle
\LO(p,\mathtt{RTs},\mathtt{Lbls})
thus consists of several subsets, each resembling a condition of
Definition 8
(Satisfaction)
in
fig.

5
.
Dependency outputs are exempt: the
Definition 11
(Enhanced LTS for Networks)
renames them to
\tau
, so the label oracle simply looks past them without requiring a dependency output action.

We now state our transparency result, after defining a final requirement: minimality of satisfaction.
This allows us to step backward through satisfaction relations, such that we can reason about buffered messages.

Definition 14
(Minimal Satisfaction)

We write
\vdash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{@}p
whenever there exists
\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\RTsOf(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p)\mathbin{@}p
(Def.
8
) and
\mathcal{R}
is
minimal
, i.e., there is no
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\vDash\mathcal{P}\triangleright\RTsOf(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p)\mathbin{@}p
.

Theorem 4.4
(Transparency)

Suppose
\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G\mathbin{@}p
(Def.
14
).
Let
\Omega:=\LO(p,\RTsOf(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G,p),\emptyset)
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\mathrel{\approx_{\Omega}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}
.

We sketch the proof of
theorem

4.4
(see
[
report/vdHeuvelPD23
]
).
The minimal satisfaction of the monitored blackbox contains all states that the monitored blackbox can reach through transitions.
We create a relation
\mathcal{B}
by pairing each such state
{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P^{\prime}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M^{\prime}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\vec{n}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}p\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}P^{\prime}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\vec{n},\vec{m}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}
—notice how the buffers are combined.
We do so while keeping an informative relation between relative types, monitors, buffers, and oracles.
This information gives us the appropriate oracles to include in
\mathcal{B}
.
We then show that
\mathcal{B}
is a weak bisimulation by proving that the initial monitored and unmonitored blackbox are in
\mathcal{B}
, and that the conditions of
definition

12
hold.
While Clause 1 is straightforward, Clause 2 requires care: by using the relation between relative types, monitors, and buffers, we infer the shape of the monitor from a transition of the unmonitored blackbox.
This allows us to show that the monitored blackbox can mimic the transition, possibly after outputting dependencies and/or receiving additional messages (as discussed above).

We close by comparing our
theorems

4.2
and
4.4
with Bocchi
et al.
’s safety and transparency results
[
7
]
, respectively.
First, their safety result
[
7
, Thm. 5.2]
guarantees satisfaction instead of assuming it; their framework suppresses unexpected messages, which prevents the informative guarantee given by our
theorem

4.2
.
Second,
theorem

4.4
and their transparency result
[
7
, Thm. 6.1]
differ, among other things, in the presence of an oracle, which is not needed in their setting: they can inspect the inputs of monitored processes, whereas we cannot verify the inputs of a blackbox without actually sending messages to it.

5
Conclusion

We have proposed a new framework for dynamically analyzing networks of communicating components (blackboxes), governed by global types, with minimal assumptions about observable behavior.
We use global types and relative projection
[
15
]
to synthesize monitors, and define when a monitored component satisfies the governing protocol.
We prove that networks of correct monitored components are sound with respect to a global type, and that monitors are transparent.

We have implemented a practical toolkit, called
RelaMon
, based on the framework presented here.

RelaMon
allows users to deploy JavaScript programs that monitor web-applications in any programming language and with third-party/closed-source components according to a global type.
The toolkit is publicly available
[
10
]
and
includes implementations of our running example (the global type
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{a}}
), as well as an example that incorporates a closed-source weather API.

As future work, we plan to extend our framework to uniformly analyze systems combining monitored blackboxes and statically checked components (following
[
15
]
).
We also plan to study under which restrictions our approach coincides with Bocchi
et al.
’s
[
7
]
.

6
The Running Example from
[
7
]

Bocchi
et al.

[
7
]
develop a running example that is similar to ours.
Their example concerns an ATM protocol between a client (
c
) and a payment server (
s
), preceded by a client authorization through a separate authenticator (
a
).

Bocchi
et al.
’s example includes assertions.
Assertions are orthogonal to the method of projecting global types onto local procotols and extracting monitors from global types; adding assertions does not modify the spirit of our approach.
Next we present
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{ATM}}
, a version of Bocchi
et al.
’s running example without assertions; it allows us to illustrate how our approach covers protocols considered in Bocchi
et al.
’s approach.

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{ATM}}

\displaystyle:=c{!}a\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{login}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.a{!}s\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{ok}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.a{!}c\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{ok}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0},\mathsf{fail}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.a{!}c\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{fail}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}

\displaystyle:=\mu X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.s{!}c\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{account}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.c{!}s\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{withdraw}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0},\mathsf{deposit}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.X\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0},\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}

Notice how, for this example to work under traditional forms of projection,
a
needs to explicitly forward the success of the login attempt to
c
.

Our framework supports
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{ATM}}
as is, because it is well-formed according to
definition

5
.
The relative projections attesting to this are as follows (cf.
algorithm

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}\mathbin{\rangle}(c,s)

\displaystyle=\mu X\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.s{!}c\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{account}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.c{!}s\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{withdraw}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.X\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42},\mathsf{deposit}\langle\mathsf{int}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.X\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42},\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}\mathbin{\rangle}(c,a)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{end}

\displaystyle=c{!}a\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{login}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.(a{!}s){!}c\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{ok}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.a{!}c\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{ok}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42},\mathsf{quit}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.a{!}c\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace

As mentioned before,
\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{ATM}}
contains an explicit dependency.
We can modify the global type to make this dependency implicit, without altering

\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ATM}}:=c{!}a\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{login}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.a{!}s\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\{\mathsf{ok}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0},\mathsf{fail}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}\}

Notice how the change has simplified the projection onto

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{c,s}:=\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ATM}}\mathbin{\rangle}(c,s)

\displaystyle=(s{?}a){!}c\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{ok}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\big(\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G_{\mathsf{loop}}\mathbin{\rangle}(c,s)\big)\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42},\mathsf{quit}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace

\displaystyle=c{!}a\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{login}\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{s,a}:=\color[rgb]{0.74,0.29,0}G^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ATM}}\mathbin{\rangle}(s,a)

\displaystyle=a{!}s\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\lBrace\mathsf{ok}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42},\mathsf{quit}\langle\rangle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}.\mathsf{end}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rBrace

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}c\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}Q_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M^{\prime}_{c}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(s,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{c,s}),(a,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{c,a})\}\mathbin{@}c

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}s\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}Q_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M^{\prime}_{s}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(c,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{c,s}),(a,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{s,a})\}\mathbin{@}s

\displaystyle\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}[}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\langle}a\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}Q_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\rangle}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}M^{\prime}_{a}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}:}\varepsilon{\color[rgb]{0.16,0.23,0.42}]}\triangleright\{(c,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{c,a}),(s,\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}R^{\prime}_{s,a})\}\mathbin{@}a
